\end{array}}\right)^{\prime}=\left({-\rho,\frac{1}{r},\cdots,\frac{1}{r}}\right)\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{p_{1}\mu_{00},}&\cdots&{,p_{1}\mu_{00}}\\
\end{array}}\right)^{\prime}=\\
p_{1}\mu_{00}\left({1-\rho}\right)\neq 0.

The expected value of the numerator of the SLANC test statistic is

\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbf{c^{\prime}}}({\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{1}-{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{0})|\,H_{0}}\right]={\mathbf{c^{\prime}}}\mathbb{E}\left[{({\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{1}-{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{0})|\,H_{0}}\right]\\
=\left.{{\mathbf{c^{\prime}}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{(p_{1}+\frac{{p_{2}p_{3}}}{\tau}t_{0})\mu_{00},}&\cdots&{,(p_{1}+\frac{{p_{2}p_{3}}}{\tau}t_{r})\mu_{00}}\\
\end{array}}\right)^{\prime}}\right|_{H_{0}:p_{3}=0}\\
={\mathbf{c^{\prime}}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{p_{1}\mu_{00},}&\cdots&{,p_{1}\mu_{00}}\\
\end{array}}\right)^{\prime}=p_{1}\mu_{00}{\mathbf{c^{\prime}}}\left({1,\ldots,1}\right)\\
=\frac{{6p_{1}\mu_{00}}}{{r(r+1)(r+2)}}\left({-\rho+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}{\left({2j-r}\right)}}\right)=\frac{{6p_{1}\mu_{00}}}{{r(r+1)(r+2)}}\left({r-\rho}\right)\neq 0

The expected value of the numerator of the SLAIN test statistic is

\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbf{c^{\prime}}}({\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{1}-{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{0})|\,H_{0}}\right]=p_{1}\mu_{00}{\mathbf{c^{\prime}}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&\cdots&1\\
\end{array}}\right)\\
=p_{1}\mu_{00}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\frac{{12j+6\rho r(2j-r-1)}}{{r(r+1)\left[{\rho r(r-1)+2(2r+1)}\right]}}}\\
=\frac{{p_{1}\mu_{00}}}{{r(r+1)\left[{\rho r(r-1)+2(2r+1)}\right]}}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{12j+6\rho r(2j-r-1)}\\
=\frac{{6p_{1}\mu_{00}(1-\rho)}}{{\left[{\rho r(r-1)+2(2r+1)}\right]}}\neq 0

Thus, ANCOVA, SLAIN and SLANC are all biased under the null and therefore not valid in observational studies under LDD.

A.3
Proof that two-stage and GLS are equivalent approaches under CS or RS for
V(t_{0})=0

In the setting where all subjects are observed at the same set of time points, this appendix will proof:

(i)

That the estimator of the difference of the rates of change in the two exposure groups obtained using the summary measure (two-stage) approach is algebraically equivalent to the estimator of
\gamma_{3}
obtained from fitting model (
2.6
) by OLS.

(ii)

That when the covariance matrix
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}={\mathbf{\Sigma}}
has a CS or RS structure, the estimators from model (
2.6
) obtained by OLS and GLS are algebraically equivalent. Given (i), this implies that the estimator from the summary measure approach is algebraically equivalent to the GLS estimator. We also show that this is not the case for DEX.

Given (i) and (ii), since the estimators from the summary measure (two-stage) approach, and GLS are the same linear combination of
\left({{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{1}-{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{0}}\right)
,
{\mathbf{d^{\prime}}}\left({{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{1}-{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{0}}\right)
, once we assume a covariance structure for
Var\left[{{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}|{\mathbf{X}}_{i}}\right]={\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}
, the test statistic for the two methods is also equivalent and equal to

T=\frac{{{\mathbf{d^{\prime}}}\left({{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{1}-{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{0}}\right)}}{{\sqrt{Var\left({{\mathbf{d^{\prime}}}\left({{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{1}-{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{0}}\right)}\right)}}},

where

Var\left({{\mathbf{d^{\prime}}}\left({{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{1}-{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{0}}\right)}\right)={\mathbf{d^{\prime}}}Var\left({{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{1}-{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{0}}\right){\mathbf{d}}\\
={\mathbf{d^{\prime}}}\left({\frac{1}{{Np_{e}}}Var\left({{\mathbf{Y}}_{i,k_{i}=1}}\right)+\frac{1}{{N(1-p_{e})}}Var\left({{\mathbf{Y}}_{i,k_{i}=0}}\right)}\right){\mathbf{d}}=\frac{{{\mathbf{d^{\prime}\Sigma d}}}}{{Np_{e}(1-p_{e})}}.

Proof of (i)

Summary measure (two-stage) approach

Let
{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}
be a
(r+1)\times 2
matrix that contains a column of ones and the column of times for participant
i
. Since all subjects are observed at the same set of time points then
{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}={\mathbf{Z}}
. Here, the summary measure is the subject-specific OLS slope associated with time from the regression of
{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}
on
{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}={\mathbf{Z}}
. Let us call
{\mathbf{\hat{\beta}}}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,N
, the
(2\times 1)
vector containing the subject-specific intercept and slope of the regression, where
{\mathbf{\hat{\beta}}}_{i}=\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Y}}_{i}
. The subject-specific intercepts and slopes are averaged in each exposure group as follows,

{\mathbf{\hat{\beta}}}_{k}=\frac{{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}{\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}}{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}\,I\left\{{k_{i}=k}\right\}}}{{n_{k}}}=\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}\frac{{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}{{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}I\left\{{k_{i}=k}\right\}}}}{{n_{k}}}=\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\bar{Y}}}_{k},

where
I\left\{{k_{i}=k}\right\}
is an indicator variable that takes the value one when
k_{i}=k
and zero otherwise;
n_{k}
is the number of participants in exposure group
k
,
k=0,1
; and
{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{k}
is the average of
{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}
in group
k
. Since we are interested in the second component of
{\mathbf{\hat{\beta}}}_{k}
, the slope associated with time, we define
\bar{S}_{k}=\left({\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}}\right)_{(2)}{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{k}
, where the subscript (2) indicates the second row of the matrix
\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}
. We are interested in the difference, which is
\left({\bar{S}_{1}-\bar{S}_{0}}\right)=\left({\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}}\right)_{(2)}\left({{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{1}-{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{0}}\right)
.

OLS approach

With the OLS approach, we fit all the data at the same time, using

\mathbb{E}\left({Y_{ij}|X_{ij}}\right)=\gamma_{0}+\gamma_{1}t_{ij}+\gamma_{2}k_{i}+\gamma_{3}\left({t_{ij}\times k_{i}}\right),

and our interest in on
\gamma_{3}
. Reparameterizing, we can fit model

\mathbb{E}\left({Y_{ij}|X_{ij}}\right)=\gamma_{0}^{*}\left({1-k_{i}}\right)+\gamma_{1}^{*}\left({1-k_{i}}\right)t_{ij}+\gamma_{2}^{*}k_{i}+\gamma_{3}^{*}k_{i}t_{ij},

and our parameter of interest is now
\gamma_{3}=\gamma_{3}^{*}-\gamma_{1}^{*}
. The OLS estimator of the latter model can be derived as

{\mathbf{\hat{\gamma}}}^{*}=\left({{\mathbf{X^{\prime}X}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{X^{\prime}Y}}=\left({\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}{{\mathbf{X^{\prime}}}_{i}{\mathbf{X}}_{i}}}\right)^{-1}\left({\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}{{\mathbf{X^{\prime}}}_{i}{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}}}\right),

where
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}
is the covariate matrix for subject
i
and can be written as
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\mathbf{Z}}&{\mathbf{0}}\\
\end{array}}\right)
if participant
i
is unexposed and
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\mathbf{0}}&{\mathbf{Z}}\\
\end{array}}\right)
if exposed. Then,

\left({\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}{{\mathbf{X^{\prime}}}_{i}{\mathbf{X}}_{i}}}\right)=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{N(1-p_{e}){\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}&{\mathbf{0}}\\
{\mathbf{0}}&{Np_{e}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\\
\end{array}}\right),

\left({\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}{{\mathbf{X^{\prime}}}_{i}{\mathbf{X}}_{i}}}\right)^{-1}=\frac{1}{N}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\frac{1}{{(1-p_{e})}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}}&{\mathbf{0}}\\
{\mathbf{0}}&{\frac{1}{{p_{e}}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}}\\
\end{array}}\right),

and

\left({\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}{{\mathbf{X^{\prime}}}_{i}{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}}}\right)=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}}\\
{\mathbf{0}}\\
\end{array}}\right)\left({\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}{{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}I\left\{{k_{i}=0}\right\}}}\right)+\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\mathbf{0}}\\
{{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}}\\
\end{array}}\right)\left({\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}{{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}I\left\{{k_{i}=1}\right\}}}\right)\\
=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{N(1-p_{e}){\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\bar{Y}}}_{0}}\\
{Np_{e}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\bar{Y}}}_{1}}\\
\end{array}}\right),

so

{\mathbf{\hat{\gamma}}}^{*}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\bar{Y}}}_{0}}\\
{\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\bar{Y}}}_{1}}\\
\end{array}}\right).

To compute
\hat{\gamma}_{3}=\hat{\gamma}_{3}^{*}-\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{*}
we need to subtract the second from the fourth component, so
\hat{\gamma}_{3}=\left({\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}}\right)_{(2)}\left({{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{1}-{\mathbf{\bar{Y}}}_{0}}\right)
as in the two-stage approach.

Proof of (ii)

A necessary and sufficient condition for the OLS and GLS estimators to be the same is
{\mathbf{HV}}={\mathbf{VH}}

[
Puntanen:1989
, condition Z5]
, where
{\mathbf{H}}
is the hat matrix
{\mathbf{H}}={\mathbf{X}}({\mathbf{X^{\prime}X}})^{-1}{\mathbf{X^{\prime}}}
,
{\mathbf{X}}
is our case the
N(r+1)\times 4
matrix of covariates based on model (
2.6
), and
{\mathbf{V}}
is the
N(r+1)\times N(r+1)
covariance matrix of
{\mathbf{Y}}
, which is a block-diagonal matrix with the diagonal blocks equal to
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
. As in the OLS derivation, we reparameterize the model as

\mathbb{E}\left({Y_{ij}|X_{ij}}\right)=\gamma_{0}^{*}\left({1-k_{i}}\right)+\gamma_{1}^{*}\left({1-k_{i}}\right)t_{ij}+\gamma_{2}^{*}k_{i}+\gamma_{3}^{*}k_{i}t_{ij},

and for convenience we sort
{\mathbf{X}}
sot that the first
N(1-p_{e})
participants are unexposed and therefore have
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\mathbf{Z}}&{\mathbf{0}}\\
\end{array}}\right)
, and the following
Np_{e}
are exposed and have
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\mathbf{0}}&{\mathbf{Z}}\\
\end{array}}\right)
. As derived in the OLS case,

({\mathbf{X^{\prime}X}})^{-1}=\frac{1}{N}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\frac{1}{{(1-p_{e})}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}}&{{\mathbf{0^{\prime}}}}\\
{\mathbf{0}}&{\frac{1}{{p_{e}}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}}\\
\end{array}}\right).

Then, it can be derived that

{\mathbf{H}}={\mathbf{X}}({\mathbf{X^{\prime}X}})^{-1}{\mathbf{X^{\prime}}}=\frac{1}{N}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{{\mathbf{H}}_{11}}&{{\mathbf{0^{\prime}}}}\\
{\mathbf{0}}&{{\mathbf{H}}_{22}}\\
\end{array}}\right),

where
{\mathbf{H}}_{11}
is a block matrix of
N(1-p_{e})\times N(1-p_{e})
blocks, each block being equal to
\frac{1}{{1-p_{e}}}{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}
; and
{\mathbf{H}}_{22}
is a block matrix with
Np_{e}\times Np_{e}
blocks, each block being equal to
\frac{1}{{p_{e}}}{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}
. Since
{\mathbf{V}}
is block diagonal with the diagonal blocks equal to
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
, it follows that
{\mathbf{HV}}
is going to be of the form

{\mathbf{HV}}=\frac{1}{N}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\left({{\mathbf{HV}}}\right)_{11}}&{{\mathbf{0^{\prime}}}}\\
{\mathbf{0}}&{\left({{\mathbf{HV}}}\right)_{22}}\\
\end{array}}\right),

where
\left({{\mathbf{HV}}}\right)_{11}
is a block matrix of
N(1-p_{e})\times N(1-p_{e})
blocks, each block being equal to
\frac{1}{{1-p_{e}}}{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\Sigma}}
; and
\left({{\mathbf{HV}}}\right)_{22}
is a block matrix with
Np_{e}\times Np_{e}
blocks, each block being equal to
\frac{1}{{p_{e}}}{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\Sigma}}
. Similarly, we can derive that
{\mathbf{VH}}
is of the form

{\mathbf{VH}}=\frac{1}{N}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\left({{\mathbf{VH}}}\right)_{11}}&{{\mathbf{0^{\prime}}}}\\
{\mathbf{0}}&{\left({{\mathbf{VH}}}\right)_{22}}\\
\end{array}}\right),

where
\left({{\mathbf{VH}}}\right)_{11}
is a block matrix of
N(1-p_{e})\times N(1-p_{e})
blocks, each block being equal to
\frac{1}{{1-p_{e}}}{\mathbf{\Sigma Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}
; and
\left({{\mathbf{VH}}}\right)_{22}
is a block matrix with
Np_{e}\times Np_{e}
blocks, each block being equal to
\frac{1}{{p_{e}}}{\mathbf{\Sigma Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}
. Clearly, then, proving that
{\mathbf{HV}}={\mathbf{VH}}
is equivalent to proving that
{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\Sigma}}={\mathbf{\Sigma Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}
.

Next, we show that the
{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\Sigma}}={\mathbf{\Sigma Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}
holds for
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
having a CS or RS structure and therefore the OLS and GLS estimators are algebraically equivalent in those cases. We also show that the condition does not hold for DEX.

CS

Under CS,
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}=\sigma^{2}\left({\rho{\mathbf{11^{\prime}}}+(1-\rho){\mathbf{I}}}\right)
, where
{\mathbf{I}}
is the
(r+1)\times(r+1)
identity matrix and
{\mathbf{1}}
a
(r+1)\times 1
vector of ones. Then,

{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\Sigma}}=\sigma^{2}{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}\left({\rho{\mathbf{11^{\prime}}}+(1-\rho){\mathbf{I}}}\right)\\
=\sigma^{2}\rho{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}11^{\prime}}}+\sigma^{2}(1-\rho){\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}.

Since
{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}
is a projection matrix in the subspace defined by columns of
{\mathbf{Z}}
, and the first column of
{\mathbf{Z}}
is
{\mathbf{1}}
, then
{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}1}}={\mathbf{1}}
and
{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\Sigma}}=\sigma^{2}\rho{\mathbf{11^{\prime}}}+\sigma^{2}(1-\rho){\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}
. Now, we derive an expression for

{\mathbf{\Sigma Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}=\sigma^{2}\left({\rho{\mathbf{11^{\prime}}}+(1-\rho){\mathbf{I}}}\right){\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}\\
=\sigma^{2}\rho{\mathbf{11^{\prime}Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}+\sigma^{2}(1-\rho){\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}.

For the same reasoning used above,
{\mathbf{1^{\prime}Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}={\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}
, and therefore
{\mathbf{\Sigma Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}=\sigma^{2}\rho{\mathbf{11^{\prime}}}+\sigma^{2}(1-\rho){\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}
, which is the same expression we derived for
{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\Sigma}}
.

RS

Under RS,
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}={\mathbf{ZDZ^{\prime}}}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{I}}
. Then,

{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\Sigma}}={\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}\left({{\mathbf{ZDZ^{\prime}}}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{I}}}\right)\\
={\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}ZDZ^{\prime}}}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}={\mathbf{ZDZ^{\prime}}}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}.

Now, we derive an expression for

{\mathbf{\Sigma Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}=\left({{\mathbf{ZDZ^{\prime}}}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{I}}}\right){\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}={\mathbf{ZDZ^{\prime}}}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}},

which is the same expression we derived for
{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\Sigma}}
.

DEX

A counterexample is enough to show that
{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\Sigma}}={\mathbf{\Sigma Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}
does not hold for DEX. With
r=2
then

{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{5/6}&{1/3}&{-1/6}\\
{1/3}&{1/3}&{1/3}\\
{-1/6}&{1/3}&{5/6}\\
\end{array}}\right).

If we take
\sigma^{2}=1
,
\rho=0.8
and
\theta=1
(AR(1) covariance structure) then

{\mathbf{\Sigma}}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&{0.8}&{0.64}\\
{0.8}&1&{0.8}\\
{0.64}&{0.8}&1\\
\end{array}}\right).

Now,

{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\Sigma}}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{5/6}&{1/3}&{-1/6}\\
{1/3}&{1/3}&{1/3}\\
{-1/6}&{1/3}&{5/6}\\
\end{array}}\right)\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&{0.8}&{0.64}\\
{0.8}&1&{0.8}\\
{0.64}&{0.8}&1\\
\end{array}}\right)\\
=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{0.993}&{0.866}&{0.633}\\
{0.813}&{0.866}&{0.813}\\
{0.633}&{0.866}&{0.993}\\
\end{array}}\right)

and

{\mathbf{\Sigma Z}}\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&{0.8}&{0.64}\\
{0.8}&1&{0.8}\\
{0.64}&{0.8}&1\\
\end{array}}\right)\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{5/6}&{1/3}&{-1/6}\\
{1/3}&{1/3}&{1/3}\\
{-1/6}&{1/3}&{5/6}\\
\end{array}}\right)\\
=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{0.993}&{0.813}&{0.633}\\
{0.866}&{0.866}&{0.866}\\
{0.633}&{0.813}&{0.993}\\
\end{array}}\right).

We can see that the the [2,1], [1,2], [3,2] and [2,3] components differ, so the condition does not hold.

A.4
Proof that
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
is the same for
r=1
and
r=2
under LDD and
V(t_{0})=0
with fixed follow-up period
\tau
and equidistant time points.

Formula (
3.4
) for the
V(t_{0})=0
case and expressed as a function of
\tau
is

{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\frac{{\left({\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{v_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\right)r^{2}}}{{p_{e}(1-p_{e})\tau^{2}\det({\mathbf{A}})}},

where the term
v_{jj^{\prime}}
is the
[j,j^{\prime}]
component of the inverse of
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
and

{\mathbf{A}}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{v_{jj^{\prime}}}}}&{\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jv_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\\
{\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jv_{jj^{\prime}}}}}&{\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jj^{\prime}v_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\\
\end{array}}\right)=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&\cdots&1\\
0&\cdots&r\\
\end{array}}\right){\mathbf{\Sigma}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&0\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
1&r\\
\end{array}}\right).

Let us call

{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{1}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sigma_{11}}&{\sigma_{1\tau}}\\
{\sigma_{1\tau}}&{\sigma_{\tau\tau}}\\
\end{array}}\right)

the covariance matrix for the case
r=1
and

{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{2}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sigma_{11}}&{\sigma_{1,\tau/2}}&{\sigma_{1,\tau}}\\
{\sigma_{1,\tau/2}}&{\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}}&{\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}\\
{\sigma_{1,\tau}}&{\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}&{\sigma_{\tau,\tau}}\\
\end{array}}\right)

for the case
r=2
and note that
\sigma_{11}
,
\sigma_{1,\tau}
and
\sigma_{\tau,\tau}
are the same in the two matrices. Let us call
{\mathbf{A}}_{1}
the matrix
{\mathbf{A}}
for the case
r=1
and
{\mathbf{A}}_{2}
for the case
r=2
. Then, the expression
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
will be the same for
r=1
and
r=2
if and only if

\frac{{{\mathbf{A}}_{1}[1,1]}}{{\det({\mathbf{A}}_{1})}}=\frac{{4{\mathbf{A}}_{2}[1,1]}}{{\det({\mathbf{A}}_{2})}}.

We can now derive

{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{1}^{-1}=\frac{1}{{\sigma_{11}\sigma_{\tau\tau}-\sigma_{1\tau}^{2}}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sigma_{11}}&{\sigma_{1\tau}}\\
{\sigma_{1\tau}}&{\sigma_{\tau\tau}}\\
\end{array}}\right)

and

{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{2}^{-1}=\frac{1}{{-2\sigma_{1,\tau}\sigma_{1,\tau/2}\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}+\sigma_{1,\tau}^{2}\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}+\sigma_{1,\tau/2}^{2}\sigma_{\tau,\tau}+\sigma_{11}\left({\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}^{2}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}\sigma_{\tau,\tau}}\right)}}\\
\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}^{2}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}\sigma_{\tau,\tau}}&&\\
{\sigma_{1,\tau/2}\sigma_{\tau,\tau}-\sigma_{1,\tau}\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}&{\sigma_{1,\tau}^{2}-\sigma_{11}\sigma_{\tau,\tau}}&\\
{\sigma_{1,\tau}\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}-\sigma_{1,\tau/2}\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}&{\sigma_{11}\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}-\sigma_{1,\tau}\sigma_{1,\tau/2}}&{\sigma_{1,\tau/2}^{2}-\sigma_{11}\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}}\\
\end{array}}\right)

Also,

{\mathbf{A}}_{1}=\frac{1}{{\sigma_{11}\sigma_{\tau\tau}-\sigma_{1\tau}^{2}}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&1\\
0&1\\
\end{array}}\right)\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sigma_{11}}&{\sigma_{1\tau}}\\
{\sigma_{1\tau}}&{\sigma_{\tau\tau}}\\
\end{array}}\right)\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&0\\
1&1\\
\end{array}}\right)\\
=\frac{1}{{\sigma_{11}\sigma_{\tau\tau}-\sigma_{1\tau}^{2}}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sigma_{11}-2\sigma_{1\tau}+\sigma_{\tau\tau}}&{\sigma_{11}-\sigma_{1\tau}}\\
{\sigma_{11}-\sigma_{1\tau}}&{\sigma_{11}}\\
\end{array}}\right),

\det({\mathbf{A}}_{1})=\frac{1}{{\sigma_{11}\sigma_{\tau\tau}-\sigma_{1\tau}^{2}}}

and

\frac{{\left({\sum\limits_{j=0}^{1}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{1}{v_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\right)}}{{\det({\mathbf{A}}_{1})}}=\frac{{{\mathbf{A}}_{1}[1,1]}}{{\det({\mathbf{A}}_{1})}}=\sigma_{11}-2\sigma_{1\tau}+\sigma_{\tau\tau};

and

{\mathbf{A}}_{2}=\frac{1}{{-2\sigma_{1,\tau}\sigma_{1,\tau/2}\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}+\sigma_{1,\tau}^{2}\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}+\sigma_{1,\tau/2}^{2}\sigma_{\tau,\tau}+\sigma_{11}\left({\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}^{2}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}\sigma_{\tau,\tau}}\right)}}\\
\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&1&1\\
0&1&2\\
\end{array}}\right)\\
\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}^{2}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}\sigma_{\tau,\tau}}&&\\
{\sigma_{1,\tau/2}\sigma_{\tau,\tau}-\sigma_{1,\tau}\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}&{\sigma_{1,\tau}^{2}-\sigma_{11}\sigma_{\tau,\tau}}&\\
{\sigma_{1,\tau}\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}-\sigma_{1,\tau/2}\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}&{\sigma_{11}\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}-\sigma_{1,\tau}\sigma_{1,\tau/2}}&{\sigma_{1,\tau/2}^{2}-\sigma_{11}\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}}\\
\end{array}}\right)\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&0\\
1&1\\
1&2\\
\end{array}}\right).

It can be derived that

\frac{{{\mathbf{A}}_{2}[1,1]}}{{\det({\mathbf{A}}_{2})}}=\frac{1}{{\sigma_{11}+2\sigma_{1,\tau}-4\left({\sigma_{1,\tau/2}+\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}}\right)+\sigma_{\tau,\tau}}}\\
\biggl\{-\sigma_{1,\tau}^{2}-\left({\sigma_{1,\tau/2}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}\right)^{2}+2\sigma_{1,\tau}\left({\sigma_{1,\tau/2}+\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}}\right)+\\
\left({\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}-2\sigma_{1,\tau/2}}\right)\sigma_{\tau,\tau}+{\sigma_{11}\left({\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}+\sigma_{\tau,\tau}-2\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}\right)}\biggr\}

Then,

\frac{{{\mathbf{A}}_{1}[1,1]}}{{\det({\mathbf{A}}_{1})}}=\frac{{4{\mathbf{A}}_{2}[1,1]}}{{\det({\mathbf{A}}_{2})}}

if and only if

\sigma_{11}-2\sigma_{1\tau}+\sigma_{\tau\tau}=\frac{4}{{\sigma_{11}+2\sigma_{1,\tau}-4\left({\sigma_{1,\tau/2}+\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}}\right)+\sigma_{\tau,\tau}}}\\
\biggl\{-\sigma_{1,\tau}^{2}-\left({\sigma_{1,\tau/2}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}\right)^{2}+2\sigma_{1,\tau}\left({\sigma_{1,\tau/2}+\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}}\right)+\\
\left({\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}-2\sigma_{1,\tau/2}}\right)\sigma_{\tau,\tau}+{\sigma_{11}\left({\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}+\sigma_{\tau,\tau}-2\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}\right)}\biggr\},

which with some algebra it reduces to
\sigma_{11}-\sigma_{\tau\tau}=2\left({\sigma_{1,\tau/2}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}\right)
. So,
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
will be the same for
r=1
and
r=2
if and only if
\sigma_{11}-\sigma_{\tau\tau}=2\left({\sigma_{1,\tau/2}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}\right)
. We can check that for the covariance structures used in the paper, i.e. compound symmetry (CS) (section
3.2
), damped exponential (DEX) (section
3.3
) and random intercepts and slopes (RS) (section
3.4
) this condition is met. For CS,

{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{2}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sigma_{11}}&{\sigma_{1,\tau/2}}&{\sigma_{1,\tau}}\\
{\sigma_{1,\tau/2}}&{\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}}&{\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}\\
{\sigma_{1,\tau}}&{\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}&{\sigma_{\tau,\tau}}\\
\end{array}}\right)=\sigma^{2}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&\rho&\rho\\
\rho&1&\rho\\
\rho&\rho&1\\
\end{array}}\right),

so
\sigma_{11}-\sigma_{\tau\tau}=\sigma^{2}\left({1-1}\right)=0
and
2\left({\sigma_{1,\tau/2}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}\right)=2\sigma^{2}(\rho-\rho)=0
and the condition holds. For DEX,

{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{2}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sigma_{11}}&{\sigma_{1,\tau/2}}&{\sigma_{1,\tau}}\\
{\sigma_{1,\tau/2}}&{\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}}&{\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}\\
{\sigma_{1,\tau}}&{\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}&{\sigma_{\tau,\tau}}\\
\end{array}}\right)=\sigma^{2}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&\rho&{\rho^{2^{\theta}}}\\
\rho&1&\rho\\
{\rho^{2^{\theta}}}&\rho&1\\
\end{array}}\right),

so
\sigma_{11}-\sigma_{\tau\tau}=\sigma^{2}\left({1-1}\right)=0
and
2\left({\sigma_{1,\tau/2}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}\right)=2\sigma^{2}(\rho-\rho)=0
and the condition holds. For RS,

{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{2}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sigma_{11}}&{\sigma_{1,\tau/2}}&{\sigma_{1,\tau}}\\
{\sigma_{1,\tau/2}}&{\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau/2}}&{\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}\\
{\sigma_{1,\tau}}&{\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}&{\sigma_{\tau,\tau}}\\
\end{array}}\right)=\\
{\footnotesize\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sigma_{b_{0}}^{2}+\sigma_{within}^{2}}&&\\
{\sigma_{b_{0}}^{2}+\rho_{b_{0}b_{1}}\sigma_{b_{0}}\sigma_{b_{1}}}&{\sigma_{b_{0}}^{2}+\sigma_{b_{1}}^{2}+2\rho_{b_{0}b_{1}}\sigma_{b_{0}}\sigma_{b_{1}}+\sigma_{within}^{2}}&\\
{\sigma_{b_{0}}^{2}+2\rho_{b_{0}b_{1}}\sigma_{b_{0}}\sigma_{b_{1}}}&{\sigma_{b_{0}}^{2}+3\rho_{b_{0}b_{1}}\sigma_{b_{0}}\sigma_{b_{1}}+2\sigma_{b_{1}}^{2}}&{\sigma_{b_{0}}^{2}+4\sigma_{b_{1}}^{2}+4\rho_{b_{0}b_{1}}\sigma_{b_{0}}\sigma_{b_{1}}+\sigma_{within}^{2}}\\
\end{array}}\right)},

so

\sigma_{11}-\sigma_{\tau\tau}=\sigma_{b_{0}}^{2}+\sigma_{within}^{2}-\sigma_{b_{0}}^{2}-4\sigma_{b_{1}}^{2}-4\rho_{b_{0}b_{1}}\sigma_{b_{0}}\sigma_{b_{1}}-\sigma_{within}^{2}=-4\sigma_{b_{1}}^{2}-4\rho_{b_{0}b_{1}}\sigma_{b_{0}}\sigma_{b_{1}}

and

2\left({\sigma_{1,\tau/2}-\sigma_{\tau/2,\tau}}\right)=2\left({\sigma_{b_{0}}^{2}+\rho_{b_{0}b_{1}}\sigma_{b_{0}}\sigma_{b_{1}}-\sigma_{b_{0}}^{2}-3\rho_{b_{0}b_{1}}\sigma_{b_{0}}\sigma_{b_{1}}-2\sigma_{b_{1}}^{2}}\right)\\
=-4\sigma_{b_{1}}^{2}-4\rho_{b_{0}b_{1}}\sigma_{b_{0}}\sigma_{b_{1}}

and the condition holds.

A.5
Effect of
p_{e}
on
r

We write

N=\frac{{\left({{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}}\right)\,\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}}}{{({\mathbf{c^{\prime}{\rm B}}}_{H_{A}})^{2}}}

as

N=\frac{{g({\mathbf{\eta}})f(r)\,\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}}}{{p_{e}(1-p_{e})({\mathbf{c^{\prime}{\rm B}}}_{H_{A}})^{2}}}

where
g({\mathbf{\eta}})
does not depend on
r
or
p_{e}
. Then we can define
r
implicitly as the value/s solving the equation
F(r)=0
, where

F(r)=\frac{{g({\mathbf{\eta}})\,\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}}}{{N({\mathbf{c^{\prime}{\rm B}}}_{H_{A}})^{2}}}-\frac{{p_{e}(1-p_{e})}}{{f(r)}}.

Using implicit differentiation and differentiating both sides of
F(r)=0
we have

\frac{{\delta F(r)}}{{\delta p_{e}}}=0,

from where we can derive
\frac{{\delta r}}{{\delta p_{e}}}
,

\frac{{\delta F(r)}}{{\delta p_{e}}}=0\Leftrightarrow\frac{{(1-2p_{e})f(r)-p_{e}(1-p_{e})f^{\prime}(r)\frac{{\delta r}}{{\delta p_{e}}}}}{{\left[{f(r)}\right]^{2}}}=0\Leftrightarrow\frac{{\delta r}}{{\delta p_{e}}}=\frac{{(1-2p_{e})f(r)}}{{p_{e}(1-p_{e})f^{\prime}(r)}}.

Then, to find the value of
p_{e}
that minimizes
r
we solve
\frac{{\delta r}}{{\delta p_{e}}}=0
, which results in the only root
p_{e}=0.5
. Since
(1-2p_{e})
is greater than zero for
p_{e}<0.5
and smaller than zero for
p_{e}>0.5
,
r
has a maximum or a minimum at
p_{e}=0.5
. The sign of
\frac{{f(r)}}{{f^{\prime}(r)}}
determines whether it is a maximum or a minimum. Since the variance
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
is always positive so it is
f(r)
, and since the variance decreases as
r
increases,
f^{\prime}(r)
is negative. Therefore
\frac{{f(r)}}{{f^{\prime}(r)}}
is negative and
\frac{{\delta r}}{{\delta p_{e}}}<0
for
p_{e}<0.5
and
\frac{{\delta r}}{{\delta p_{e}}}>0
for
p_{e}>0.5
, implying that
r
is minimum at
p_{e}=0.5
.

A.6
Limit of
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
when
r\to\infty

A.6.1
CMD, CS

The inverse of a CS matrix has diagonal elements

\frac{1}{{\sigma^{2}}}\frac{{1+\rho(r-2)-\rho^{2}(r-1)}}{{(1-\rho)^{2}\left({1+r\rho}\right)}}

and off-diagonal elements

\frac{1}{{\sigma^{2}}}\frac{{-\rho}}{{(1-\rho)\left({1+r\rho}\right)}}.

The sum of a row or a column of the inverse is

\frac{1}{{\sigma^{2}}}\left({\frac{{1+\rho(r-2)-\rho^{2}(r-1)}}{{(1-\rho)^{2}\left({1+r\rho}\right)}}-\frac{{r\rho}}{{(1-\rho)\left({1+r\rho}\right)}}}\right)=\frac{1}{{\sigma^{2}\left({1+r\rho}\right)}}

and therefore

\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{v_{jj^{\prime}}}}=\frac{{r+1}}{{\sigma^{2}(1+r\rho)}}.

Also,

\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jv_{jj^{\prime}}}}=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{j\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{v_{jj^{\prime}}}}=\frac{{r(r+1)}}{{2\sigma^{2}\left({1+r\rho}\right)}}

since
\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{v_{jj^{\prime}}}
is the sum of a row or column of the inverse. We can also derive

\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jj^{\prime}v_{jj^{\prime}}}}=\frac{{r(r+1)(2+r(4+(r-1)\rho))}}{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho)\left({1+r\rho}\right)}}.

Then,

\det({\mathbf{A}})=\left({\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{v_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\right)\left({\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jj^{\prime}v_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\right)-\left({\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jv_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\right)^{2}=\frac{{r(r+1)^{2}(r+2)}}{{12\sigma^{4}(1-\rho)\left({1+r\rho}\right)}}.

Plugging in all these expressions in to equation (
3.2
), we have that under CMD and CS

{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\frac{{\sigma^{2}(1+r\rho)\left({r(r+2)(1+r\rho)s^{2}+12(1-\rho)V\left({t_{0}}\right)}\right)}}{{p_{e}(1-p_{e})(r+1)\left({r(r+2)(1+r\rho)s^{2}+12(1-\rho)\left({1-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}}\right)V\left({t_{0}}\right)}\right)}}.

Then, using the highest order terms of
r
on the numerator and denominator of
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
we can derive that

\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;\frac{{\sigma^{2}(1+r\rho)\left({r(r+2)(1+r\rho)s^{2}}\right)}}{{p_{e}(1-p_{e})(r+1)\left({r(r+2)(1+r\rho)s^{2}}\right)}}=\\
\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\frac{{\sigma^{2}(1+r\rho)}}{{p_{e}(1-p_{e})(r+1)}}\;=\frac{{\sigma^{2}\rho}}{{p_{e}(1-p_{e})}}.

A.6.2
LDD, CS

Applying the results derived in Appendix
A.6.1
to equation (
3.4
), we can derive
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
as

{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\frac{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho){\kern 1.0pt}(1-r\rho)}}{{N{\kern 1.0pt}p_{e}(1-p_{e})(r+1)\left({r(r+2)(1+r\rho)\,s^{2}+12(1-\rho)\left({1-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}}\right)V(t_{0})}\right)}}.

Since the denominator is a polynomial of fourth degree of
r
while the numerator is of first degree, then
\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;\;{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=0
.

A.6.3
CMD, AR(1)

Fixed
s
:

The AR(1) covariance matrix is given by (
3.8
) with
\theta=1
, and its inverse is a tridiagonal matrix with the form

{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{-1}=\frac{1}{{\left({1-\rho^{2s}}\right)\sigma^{2}}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&{-\rho^{s}}&0&0&\cdots&0\\
{-\rho^{s}}&{1+\rho^{2s}}&{-\rho^{s}}&0&&0\\
0&{-\rho^{s}}&{1+\rho^{2s}}&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ddots&\ddots&{-\rho^{s}}&0\\
\vdots&&\ddots&{-\rho^{s}}&{1+\rho^{2s}}&{-\rho^{s}}\\
0&0&\cdots&0&{-\rho^{s}}&1\\
\end{array}}\right)

[
Graybill:1983
, page 201]
. To use equation (
3.2
) we need to derive
\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{v_{jj^{\prime}}}}
,
\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jv_{jj^{\prime}}}}
and
\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jj^{\prime}v_{jj^{\prime}}}}
. In can be easily shown that

\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{v_{jj^{\prime}}}}=\frac{{(1+r+\rho^{s}-r\rho^{s})}}{{\sigma^{2}{\kern 1.0pt}(1+\rho^{s})}}.

Also,

\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{j\cdot v_{jj^{\prime}}}}=\frac{{r\left({1-\rho^{s}}\right)\left({1+r\left({1-\rho^{s}}\right)+\rho^{s}}\right)}}{{2\left({1-\rho^{2s}}\right)\sigma^{2}}}

and

\left({\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jj^{\prime}v_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\right)=\frac{r}{{6\left({1-\rho^{2s}}\right)\sigma^{2}}}\left({1+4\rho^{s}+\rho^{2s}+3r\left({1-\rho^{2s}}\right)+2r^{2}\left({1-\rho^{s}}\right)^{2}}\right).

If
V\left({t_{0}}\right)=0
, we can use formula (
3.3
) and

{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\frac{{\sigma^{2}(1+\rho^{s})}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}p_{e}(1-p_{e})(1+r+\rho^{s}-r\rho^{s})}}

as in Table
1
. This formula has a polynomial of first order degree of
r
in the denominator, and no terms involving
r
in the numerator. Therefore,
\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;\;{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=0
. If
V\left({t_{0}}\right)>0
the formula is very long
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
and we used Mathematica
[
Wolfram:2005
]
to get the formula and compute the limit, which was zero. Therefore,
\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;\;{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=0
also for the case of
V\left({t_{0}}\right)>0
.

Fixed
\tau

For the fixed
\tau
case we need to substitute
s
by
\tau/r
. So, for the case of
V\left({t_{0}}\right)=0
,

{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\frac{{\sigma^{2}(1+\rho^{\tau/r})}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}p_{e}(1-p_{e})(1+r+\rho^{\tau/r}-r\rho^{\tau/r})}}.

We want to compute

\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;\;{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;\frac{{\sigma^{2}(1+\rho^{\tau/r})}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}p_{e}(1-p_{e})(1+r+\rho^{\tau/r}-r\rho^{\tau/r})}}=\\
\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;\frac{{\sigma^{2}(1+\rho^{\tau/r})}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\left[{1+\rho^{\tau/r}+r(1-\rho^{\tau/r})}\right]}}.

By l’Hôpital’s rule it can be shown that
\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;r(1-\rho^{\tau/r})=-\tau\log\rho
, and then

\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;\;{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\frac{{2\sigma^{2}}}{{p_{e}(1-p_{e})\left[{2-\tau\log\rho}\right]}}.

If
V\left({t_{0}}\right)>0
, we used Mathematica
[
Wolfram:2005
]
to derive the limit, which in this case has a very complicated expression,

2\sigma^{2}\left({\left({\tau^{3}+12V(t_{0})\tau}\right)\left({\log(\rho)}\right)^{2}-6\left({\tau^{2}+4V(t_{0})}\right)\log(\rho)+12\tau}\right)\\
\left[{p_{\text{e}}(1-p_{\text{e}})(2-\tau\log(\rho))}\right]^{-1}\\
\biggl\{\left({\tau^{3}+12V(t_{0})\tau}\right)\left({\log(\rho)}\right)^{2}-12V(t_{0})(\tau\log(\rho)-2)\rho_{e,t_{0}}^{2}\log(\rho)\\
-6\left({\tau^{2}+4V(t_{0})}\right)\log(\rho)+12\tau\biggr\}^{-1}

A.6.4
LDD, AR(1)

Fixed
s

Using the results from Appendix
A.6.3
and applying formula (
3.4
) for the case
V\left({t_{0}}\right)=0
, we can derive that

{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\frac{{12\sigma^{2}{\kern 1.0pt}(1-\rho^{2s})\,\left[{\,r\,s^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})}\right]^{-1}}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}\;\,(2+r(r+3)+8\rho^{s}-2r^{2}\rho^{s}+(r-2)(r-1)\rho^{2s})}}

as is shown in Table
1
. Since the denominator is a polynomial of second degree of
r
while the has no terms involving
r
, then
\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;\;{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=0
. If
V\left({t_{0}}\right)>0
, we used Mathematica
[
Wolfram:2005
]
to derive that the limit was also zero.

Fixed
\tau

For the fixed
\tau
case we need to substitute
s
by
\tau/r
. So, for the case of
V\left({t_{0}}\right)=0
, we have

{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\frac{{12\sigma^{2}{\kern 1.0pt}(1-\rho^{2\tau/r})\,r\,\left[{\,\tau^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})}\right]^{-1}}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}\;\,(2+r(r+3)+8\rho^{\tau/r}-2r^{2}\rho^{\tau/r}+(r-2)(r-1)\rho^{2\tau/r})}},

as shown in Table
1
. This expression can be rewritten as

\frac{{12\sigma^{2}{\kern 1.0pt}(1-\rho^{2{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}})\,\left[{p_{e}(1-p_{e})}\right]^{-1}}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}\;\,\tau^{2}\left[{r\left({1-\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}\right)^{2}+\left({3\left({1-\rho^{2{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}\right)}\right)+\frac{1}{r}\left({2+8\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}+2\rho^{2{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}\right)}\right]}}.

Then, to compute
\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;\;{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
we note that the limit of the numerator is
12\sigma^{2}\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;(1-\rho^{2{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}})=0
. In the denominator, the limit of last two terms is zero, and l’Hôpital’s rule can be used to derive that the limit of the first term is also zero. Thus, we apply l’Hôpital’s rule to derive the limit of
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
, where the derivative of the numerator is

\frac{{24\sigma^{2}{\kern 1.0pt}\rho^{2{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}\tau\log\rho}}{{r^{2}}},

the derivative of the first term of the denominator is

\tau^{2}\left[{\left({1-\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}\right)^{2}+\frac{{2\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}\left({1-\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}\right)\tau\log\rho}}{r}}\right],

the derivative of the second term of the denominator is

3\tau^{2}\left[{\frac{{2{\kern 1.0pt}\rho^{2{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}\tau\log\rho}}{{r^{2}}}}\right],

and the derivative of the third term of the denominator is

2\tau^{2}\left[{\frac{{-1-4\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}-\rho^{{{2\tau}\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{{2\tau}r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}}{{r^{2}}}-\frac{{2\tau\log\rho\left({2\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}+\rho^{{{2\tau}\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{{2\tau}r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}\right)}}{{r^{3}}}}\right].

Simplifying terms,

\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;\;{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\\
{24\sigma^{2}{\kern 1.0pt}\rho^{2{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}\tau\log\rho\left[{p_{e}(1-p_{e})}\right]^{-1}\tau^{-2}}\\
\biggl\{r^{2}\left({1-\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}\right)^{2}+2r\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}\left({1-\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}\right)\tau\log\rho+3\left[{2\rho^{2{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}\tau\log\rho}\right]\\
+2\left({-1-4\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}-\rho^{2{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}\right)-\frac{{4\tau\log\rho\left({2\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}+\rho^{2{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}\right)}}{r}\biggr\}^{-1}

Now the limit of the numerator is
24\sigma^{2}{\kern 1.0pt}\tau\log\rho
. In the denominator, we need to evaluate several terms. The limit of
r^{2}\left({1-\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}\right)^{2}
can be obtained by applying l’Hôpital’s rule twice and it equals
\tau^{2}\left({\log\rho}\right)^{2}
. The limit of
2r\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}\left({1-\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}\right)\tau\log\rho
is obtained by applying l’Hôpital’s rule and it equals
-2\tau^{2}\left({\log\rho}\right)^{2}
. The limit of
3\left[{2\rho^{2{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}\tau\log\rho}\right]
is
3\left[{2\tau\log\rho}\right]
, the limit of
2\left({-1-4\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}-\rho^{2{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}\right)
is
-12
and the limit of

\frac{{4\tau\log\rho\left({2\rho^{{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}+\rho^{2{\tau\mathord{\left/{\vphantom{\tau r}}\right.\kern-1.2pt}r}}}\right)}}{r}

is zero. Therefore, with some algebra we can deduce that

\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;\;{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\frac{{24\sigma^{2}{\kern 1.0pt}\log\rho}}{{p_{e}(1-p_{e})\left[{-12\tau+6\tau^{2}\log\rho-\tau^{3}\left({\log\rho}\right)^{2}}\right]}}.

If
V\left({t_{0}}\right)>0
, using the expression derived for fixed
s
and substituting
s
by
\tau/r
, we used Mathematica
[
Wolfram:2005
]
to derive the limit, which has a complicated expression,

{24\sigma^{2}\log(\rho)\left[{p_{\text{e}}(1-p_{\text{e}})}\right]}\\
\biggl\{-\left({\tau^{3}+12V(t_{0})\tau}\right)\left({\log(\rho)}\right)^{2}+12V(t_{0})(\tau\log(\rho)-2)\rho_{e,t_{0}}^{2}\log(\rho)\\
+6\left({\tau^{2}+4V(t_{0})}\right)\log(\rho)-12\tau\biggr\}^{-1}

A.6.5
CMD, RS,
V(t_{0})=0

Since for the case
V(t_{0})>0
we need to use numerical methods to compute
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
, we only compute the limits of
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
for the case
V(t_{0})=0
. The covariance matrix of the repeated measurements is expressed as
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}={\mathbf{Z}}_{i}{\mathbf{DZ^{\prime}}}_{i}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{I}}
, and since
V(t_{0})=0
we have
{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}={\mathbf{Z}}
and then we
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}={\mathbf{\Sigma}}={\mathbf{ZDZ^{\prime}}}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{I}}
. The matrix
{\mathbf{Z}}
is
(r+1)\times 2
and contains a column of ones and the column of times (
sj,j=0,\ldots,r
). Note that formula (
3.2
) depends on

{\mathbf{A}}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{v_{jj^{\prime}}}}}&{\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jv_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\\
{\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jv_{jj^{\prime}}}}}&{\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jj^{\prime}v_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\\
\end{array}}\right)

only through
s^{2}\det({\mathbf{A}})
. For convenience in this proof we define the new matrix

{\mathbf{\tilde{A}}}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{v_{jj^{\prime}}}}}&{s\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jv_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\\
{s\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jv_{jj^{\prime}}}}}&{s^{2}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r}{\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{r}{jj^{\prime}v_{jj^{\prime}}}}}\\
\end{array}}\right)

so that
{\mathbf{\tilde{A}}}
is actually
{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\Sigma}}^{-1}{\mathbf{Z}}
. Note that
\det\left({{\mathbf{\tilde{A}}}}\right)=s^{2}\det({\mathbf{A}})
. Then, we have that
{\mathbf{\tilde{A}}}={\mathbf{Z^{\prime}\Sigma}}^{-1}{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}\left({{\mathbf{ZDZ^{\prime}}}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{I}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z}}
. Using the property

({\mathbf{ABA}}^{\prime}+{\mathbf{C}})^{-1}={\mathbf{C}}^{-1}-{\mathbf{C}}^{-1}{\mathbf{A}}\left({{\mathbf{B}}^{-1}+{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}{\mathbf{C}}^{-1}{\mathbf{A}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}{\mathbf{C}}^{-1},

which can be found in
[
Timm:2002
, property 8, page 46]
, we have that

\left({{\mathbf{ZDZ^{\prime}}}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{I}}}\right)^{-1}=\frac{1}{{\sigma_{within}^{2}}}{\mathbf{I}}-\frac{1}{{\sigma_{within}^{2}}}{\mathbf{IZ}}\left({{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}+{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{{\sigma_{within}^{2}}}{\mathbf{IZ}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}I}}\frac{1}{{\sigma_{within}^{2}}}\\
=\frac{1}{{\sigma_{within}^{2}}}{\mathbf{I}}-\frac{1}{{\sigma_{within}^{4}}}{\mathbf{Z}}\left({{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}+\frac{1}{{\sigma_{within}^{2}}}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}.

Now,

{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}\left({{\mathbf{ZDZ^{\prime}}}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{I}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z}}\\
=\frac{1}{{\sigma_{within}^{2}}}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}-\frac{1}{{\sigma_{within}^{4}}}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}\left({{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}+\frac{1}{{\sigma_{within}^{2}}}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}

and using the property

{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}-{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}\left({{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}+{\mathbf{B}}^{-1}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}=({\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}})^{-1},

which can be found in
[
Timm:2002
, property 6, page 46]
, we have that

{\mathbf{\tilde{A}}}={\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}\left({{\mathbf{ZDZ^{\prime}}}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{I}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z}}\\
=\frac{1}{{\sigma_{within}^{2}}}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}-\frac{1}{{\sigma_{within}^{4}}}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}\left({{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}+\frac{1}{{\sigma_{within}^{2}}}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}=\left({\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}\sigma_{within}^{2}+{\mathbf{D}}}\right)^{-1}.

Now,

\left({\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}\sigma_{within}^{2}+{\mathbf{D}}}\right)^{-1}=\\
\left({\sigma_{within}^{2}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\frac{{2+4r}}{{(r+1)(r+2)}}}&{\frac{{-6}}{{s(r+1)(r+2)}}}\\
{\frac{{-6}}{{s(r+1)(r+2)}}}&{\frac{{12}}{{s^{2}r(r+1)(r+2)}}}\\
\end{array}}\right)+\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sigma_{b_{0}}^{2}}&{\rho_{b_{0}b_{1}}\sigma_{b_{0}}\sigma_{b_{1}}}\\
{\rho_{b_{0}b_{1}}\sigma_{b_{0}}\sigma_{b_{1}}}&{\sigma_{b_{1}}^{2}}\\
\end{array}}\right)}\right)^{-1}

We computed this inverse using Mathematica
[
Wolfram:2005
]
, and then using equation (
3.3
) substituting
s^{2}\det({\mathbf{A}})
by
\det\left({{\mathbf{\tilde{A}}}}\right)
we derived the expression for
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
, which is

\frac{{\left({\sigma_{0}^{2}+\frac{{2(2r+1)\sigma_{w}^{2}}}{{r^{2}+3r+2}}}\right)\left({\sigma_{1}^{2}+\frac{{12\sigma_{w}^{2}}}{{\left({r^{3}+3r^{2}+2r}\right)s^{2}}}}\right)-\left({\sigma_{01}-\frac{{6\sigma_{w}^{2}}}{{\left({r^{2}+3r+2}\right)s}}}\right)^{2}}}{{(1-p_{e})p_{e}\left({\sigma_{1}^{2}+\frac{{12\sigma_{w}^{2}}}{{\left({r^{3}+3r^{2}+2r}\right)s^{2}}}}\right)}}.

The limit of this expression is

\frac{{\sigma_{0}^{2}\sigma_{1}^{2}-\sigma_{01}^{2}}}{{p_{e}(1-p_{e})\sigma_{1}^{2}}}.

Equivalently, we derived the same results for the fixed
\tau
case. This limit can be rewritten in terms of our paramterization as

\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;\;{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\frac{{\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2}\rho_{t_{0}}\left({1-\rho_{01}^{2}}\right)}}{{p_{e}(1-p_{e})}}.

A.6.6
LDD, RS,
V(t_{0})=0

When
V(t_{0})=0
, using equation (
3.4
), we can derive
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
by substituting
s^{2}\det({\mathbf{A}})
by
\det\left({{\mathbf{\tilde{A}}}}\right)
to obtain, in terms of our parameterization,

{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\left({\frac{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho_{t_{0}})}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}s^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\,}}}\right)\left({\frac{1}{{\,r(r+1)(r+2)}}+\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{1}{{\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}}\right),

as in Table
1
. Then, it is easily derived that

\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\;\;{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}=\left({\frac{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho_{t_{0}})}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}s^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\,}}}\right)\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{1}{{\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}},

and, equivalently, for the fixed
\tau
case the limit is

\left({\frac{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho_{t_{0}})}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}\tau^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\,}}}\right)\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{{\tilde{r}}}{{\,(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}.

A.7
The effect of covariance parameters on the minimum
r
for a fixed
N
, subject to power
\pi

A.7.1
The effect of
\rho
and
\rho_{t_{0}}

A.7.1.1
CMD, CS,
V(t_{0})=0

From equation (
3.6
),

r=\frac{{\beta_{2}^{2}N\,p_{e}(1-p_{e})-\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}\sigma^{2}}}{{\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}\sigma^{2}\rho-\beta_{2}^{2}N\,p_{e}(1-p_{e})}}.

Differentiating with respect to
\rho
, we get

\frac{{\partial r}}{{\partial\rho}}=\frac{{\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}\sigma^{2}\left({-\beta_{2}^{2}N\,p_{e}(1-p_{e})+\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}\sigma^{2}}\right)}}{{\left({\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}\sigma^{2}\rho-\beta_{2}^{2}N\,p_{e}(1-p_{e})}\right)^{2}}}.

If
\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}\sigma^{2}>\beta_{2}^{2}N\,p_{e}(1-p_{e})
, then
\frac{{\partial r}}{{\partial\rho}}>0
, so r increases as
\rho
increases.
If
\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}\sigma^{2}<\beta_{2}^{2}N\,p_{e}(1-p_{e})
, then
\frac{{\partial r}}{{\partial\rho}}<0
, so r decreases as
\rho
increases.

A.7.1.2
LDD, CS, fixed s,
V(t_{0})=0

The minimum
r
for fixed
N
and fixed power,
\pi
, solves

N=\frac{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho)\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}{\kern 1.0pt}}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}\gamma_{3}^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\;s^{2}\,r\,(r+1)(r+2)}},

which was obtained plugging in the corresponding value of
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
in Table
1
into equation (
3.5
). Defining

F\left({r,\,\rho}\right)=\frac{{N\gamma_{3}^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\;s^{2}\,}}{{12\sigma^{2}\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}}}-\frac{{(1-\rho)}}{{r\,(r+1)(r+2)}},

the equation
F\left({r,\,\rho}\right)=0
implicitly defines the function
r=f(\rho)
. Using implicit differentiation and taking into account that r is a function of
\rho
,
r(\rho)
, we obtain

\frac{{\partial r}}{{\partial\rho}}=\frac{{-r\,\left({r+1}\right)\left({r+2}\right)}}{{(1-\rho)(3r^{2}+6r+2)}}.

Since r is positive, the derivative is always negative, and r decreases as
\rho
increases.

A.7.1.3
LDD, CS, fixed
\tau
,
V(t_{0})=0

The minimum
r
for fixed
N
and fixed power,
\pi
, solves

N=\frac{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho)\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}{\kern 1.0pt}r}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}\gamma_{3}^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\;\tau^{2}\,\,(r+1)(r+2)}},

which was obtained plugging in the corresponding value of
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
in Table
1
into equation (
3.5
). Defining

F\left({r,\,\rho}\right)=\frac{{N\gamma_{3}^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\;\tau^{2}\,}}{{12\sigma^{2}\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}}}-\frac{{(1-\rho)r}}{{\,(r+1)(r+2)}},

the equation
F\left({r,\,\rho}\right)=0
implicitly defines the function
r=f(\rho)
. Using implicit differentiation and taking into account that r is a function of
\rho
, we obtain

\frac{{\partial r}}{{\partial\rho}}=\frac{{r\left({r+1}\right)\left({r+2}\right)}}{{(1-\rho)\left({-r^{2}+2}\right)}}.

If
r\geqslant 2
, then
\frac{{\partial r}}{{\partial\rho}}<0
. So if we are taking at least two post-baseline measures, larger values of
\rho
lead to smaller values of r to achieve the specified power. Since
\frac{r}{{\left({r+1}\right)\left({r+2}\right)}}
is the same for
r=1
and
r=2
, it is preferable to choose
r=1
since fewer measurements need to be collected. Therefore, the choice between
r=1
and
r=2
is not affected by
\rho
.

A.7.1.4
LDD, RS, fixed s,
V(t_{0})=0

The minimum
r
for fixed
N
and fixed power,
\pi
, solves

N=\frac{{\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}\left({\frac{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho_{t_{0}})}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}s^{2}\,}}}\right)\left({\frac{1}{{\,r(r+1)(r+2)}}+\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{1}{{\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}}\right)}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}\gamma_{3}^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\;}},

which was obtained by plugging in the corresponding value of
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
in Table
1
into equation (
3.5
). Defining

F\left({r,\,\rho_{t_{0}}}\right)=\\
\frac{{N\gamma_{3}^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})s^{2}}}{{12\sigma^{2}\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}}}-(1-\rho_{t_{0}})\left({\frac{1}{{\,r(r+1)(r+2)}}+\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{1}{{\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}}\right),

the equation
F\left({r,\,\rho_{t_{0}}}\right)=0
implicitly defines the function
r=f(\rho_{t_{0}})
. Using implicit differentiation and taking into account that r depends on
\rho_{t_{0}}
, we obtain

\frac{{\partial r}}{{\partial\rho_{t_{0}}}}=\frac{{-r\,\left({r+1}\right)\left({r+2}\right)\left[{\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}r\,\left({r+1}\right)\left({r+2}\right)+\left({1-\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}\right)\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}\right]}}{{\left({1-\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}\right)\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)(1-\rho_{t_{0}})\left({3r^{2}+6r+2}\right)}}<0.

Since the derivative is always negative when
r>0
, r decreases as
\rho_{t_{0}}
increases.

A.7.1.5
LDD, RS, fixed
\tau
,
V(t_{0})=0

The minimum
r
for fixed
N
and fixed power,
\pi
, solves

N=\frac{{\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}\left({\frac{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho_{t_{0}})}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}\tau^{2}\,}}}\right)\left({\frac{r}{{\,(r+1)(r+2)}}+\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{{\tilde{r}}}{{\,(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}}\right)}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}\gamma_{3}^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\;}},

which was obtained by plugging in the corresponding value of
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
in Table
1
into equation (
3.5
). Defining

F\left({r,\,\rho}\right)=\\
\frac{{N\gamma_{3}^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\tau^{2}}}{{12\sigma^{2}\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}}}-(1-\rho_{t_{0}})\left({\frac{r}{{\,(r+1)(r+2)}}+\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{{\tilde{r}}}{{\,(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}}\right),

the equation
F\left({r,\,\rho_{t_{0}}}\right)=0
implicitly defines the function
r=f(\rho_{t_{0}})
. Using implicit differentiation, and taking into account that r depends on
\rho_{t_{0}}
, we obtain

\frac{{\partial r}}{{\partial\rho_{t_{0}}}}=\frac{{\left({r+1}\right)\left({r+2}\right)\left[{\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}\tilde{r}\left({r+1}\right)\left({r+2}\right)+\left({1-\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}\right)(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)r}\right]}}{{\left({1-\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}\right)(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)\left({1-\rho_{t_{0}}}\right)(2-r^{2})}}.

If
r\geqslant 2
then
\frac{{\partial r}}{{\partial\rho_{t_{0}}}}<0
. So if we are taking at least two post-baseline measures, larger values of
\rho_{t_{0}}
lead to smaller minimal values of r to achieve a certain power. Since
\frac{r}{{\left({r+1}\right)\left({r+2}\right)}}
is the same for
r=1
and
r=2
, the resulting power of both studies would be the same and it would be preferable to choose
r=1
since less measurements need to be collected. The choice between
r=1
and
r=2
is not affected by
\rho_{t_{0}}
.

A.7.2
The effect of
\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}

A.7.2.1
LDD, RS, fixed s,
V(t_{0})=0

The minimum
r
for fixed
N
and fixed power,
\pi
, solves

N=\frac{{\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}\left({\frac{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho_{t_{0}})}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}s^{2}\,}}}\right)\left({\frac{1}{{\,r(r+1)(r+2)}}+\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{1}{{\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}}\right)}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}\gamma_{3}^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\;}},

which was obtained plugging in the corresponding value of
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
in Table
1
into equation (
3.5
). Defining

F\left({r,\,\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}\right)=\\
\frac{{N\gamma_{3}^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})s^{2}}}{{12\sigma^{2}\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}(1-\rho_{t_{0}})}}-\left({\frac{1}{{\,r(r+1)(r+2)}}+\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{1}{{\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}}\right),

the equation
F\left({r,\,\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}\right)=0
implicitly defines the function
r=f(\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}})
. Using implicit differentiation, and taking into account that r depends on
\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}
, we obtain

\frac{{\partial r}}{{\partial\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}=\frac{{r^{2}(r+1)^{2}(r+2)^{2}}}{{\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)(1-\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}})^{2}(3r^{2}+6r+2)}}>0.

Since the derivative is always positive, r increases as
\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}
increases.

A.7.2.2
LDD, RS, fixed
\tau
,
V(t_{0})=0

The minimum
r
for fixed
N
and fixed power,
\pi
, solves

N=\frac{{\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}\left({\frac{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho_{t_{0}})}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}\tau^{2}\,}}}\right)\left({\frac{r}{{\,(r+1)(r+2)}}+\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{{\tilde{r}}}{{\,(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}}\right)}}{{{\kern 1.0pt}\gamma_{3}^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\;}},

which was obtained plugging in the corresponding value of
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
in Table
1
into equation (
3.5
). Defining

F\left({r,\,\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}\right)=\\
\frac{{N\gamma_{3}^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\tau^{2}}}{{12\sigma^{2}\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}(1-\rho_{t_{0}})}}-\left({\frac{r}{{\,(r+1)(r+2)}}+\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{{\tilde{r}}}{{\,(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}}\right),

the equation
F\left({r,\,\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}\right)=0
implicitly defines the function
r=f(\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}})
. Using implicit differentiation, and taking into account that r depends on
\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}
, we obtain

\frac{\partial}{{\partial\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}}=\frac{{\tilde{r}(r+1)^{2}(r+2)^{2}}}{{\,(r^{2}-2)(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)(1-\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}})^{2}}}.

If
r\geqslant 2
,
\frac{{\partial r}}{{\partial\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}}>0
. So if we are taking at least two post-baseline measurements, the effect of increasing
\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}
is to increase the minimum r needed to achieve a pre-specified power. Since
\frac{r}{{\left({r+1}\right)\left({r+2}\right)}}
is the same for
r=1
and
r=2
, the resulting power of both studies would be the same and it is therefore preferable to choose
r=1
since less measurements need to be collected. The choice between
r=1
and
r=2
is not affected by
\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}
.

A.8
Calculation of the variance under RS and
V(t_{0})>0
assuming
t_{0i}
are normally distributed

We need to derive

{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{\rm B}=\left({\mathbb{E}\left({{\mathbf{X^{\prime}}}_{i}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}{\mathbf{X}}_{i}}\right)}\right)^{-1}.

When
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}={\mathbf{\Sigma}}
for all subjects,
\mathbb{E}\left({{\mathbf{X^{\prime}}}_{i}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}{\mathbf{X}}_{i}}\right)
can be computed exactly. This will happen when
V(t_{0})=0
, and then equations (
3.3
) and (
3.4
) for
V(t_{0})=0
provide general expression for
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
for CMD and LDD, respectively. However, if
V(t_{0})>0
then
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}\neq{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
under RS. Specifically,
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}
depends on
t_{0i}
, so we have
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(t_{0i})
. The formula for
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}
under RS is
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}={\mathbf{Z}}_{i}{\mathbf{DZ^{\prime}}}_{i}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{I}}
, where

{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}_{i}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&1\\
{t_{0i}}&\cdots&{t_{0i}+js}&\cdots&{t_{0i}+rs}\\
\end{array}}\right)

and

{\mathbf{D}}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\sigma_{0}^{2}}&{\sigma_{01}}\\
{\sigma_{01}}&{\sigma_{1}^{2}}\\
\end{array}}\right).

At this point it is convenient to introduce a new matrix

{\mathbf{W}}_{i}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&0&{k_{i}}&0\\
0&1&0&{k_{i}}\\
\end{array}}\right).

Note that under LDD,

{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}{\mathbf{W}}_{i}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&{t_{0i}}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
\vdots&{t_{0i}+js}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
1&{t_{0i}+rs}\\
\end{array}}\right)\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&0&{k_{i}}&0\\
0&1&0&{k_{i}}\\
\end{array}}\right)=\\
\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&{t_{0i}}&{k_{i}}&{t_{0i}k_{i}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\vdots&{t_{0i}+js}&\vdots&{\left({t_{0i}+js}\right)k_{i}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
1&{t_{0i}+rs}&{k_{i}}&{\left({t_{0i}+rs}\right)k_{i}}\\
\end{array}}\right)={\mathbf{X}}_{i}.

Therefore,

{\mathbf{X}}_{i}^{\prime}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}{\mathbf{X}}_{i}={\mathbf{W}}_{i}^{\prime}{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}^{\prime}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}{\mathbf{W}}_{i}={\mathbf{W}}_{i}^{\prime}{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}^{\prime}\left({{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}{\mathbf{DZ^{\prime}}}_{i}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{I}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}{\mathbf{W}}_{i}.

In Appendix
A.6.5
we derived ,

{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}\left({{\mathbf{ZDZ^{\prime}}}+\sigma_{within}^{2}{\mathbf{I}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{Z}}=\left({\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}\sigma_{within}^{2}+{\mathbf{D}}}\right)^{-1},

so we can deduce
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}^{\prime}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}{\mathbf{X}}_{i}={\mathbf{W}}_{i}^{\prime}\left({\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}\sigma_{within}^{2}+{\mathbf{D}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{W}}_{i}
. Now,

\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}_{i}{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}}\right)=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{r+1}&{\left({r+1}\right)t_{0i}+\frac{{s{\kern 1.0pt}r(r+1)}}{2}}\\
{\left({r+1}\right)t_{0i}+\frac{{s{\kern 1.0pt}r(r+1)}}{2}}&{\left({r+1}\right)t_{0i}^{2}+st_{0i}r(r+1)+\frac{{s^{2}r(r+1)(2r+1)}}{6}}\\
\end{array}}\right),

and

\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}}}_{i}{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}}\right)^{-1}=\frac{1}{{r(r+1)(r+2)s^{2}}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{2\left({r\left({1+2r}\right)s^{2}+6r{\kern 1.0pt}st_{0i}+6t_{0i}^{2}}\right)}&{-6\left({r{\kern 1.0pt}s+2t_{0i}}\right)}\\
{-6\left({r{\kern 1.0pt}s+2t_{0i}}\right)}&{12}\\
\end{array}}\right).

Using this result we computed

\left({\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}\sigma_{within}^{2}+{\mathbf{D}}}\right)^{-1}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{a(t_{0i})}&{c(t_{0i})}\\
{c(t_{0i})}&{d(t_{0i})}\\
\end{array}}\right),

where

a(t_{0i})=\frac{{\left({\frac{{12\sigma_{w}^{2}}}{{r(r+1)(r+2)s^{2}}}+\sigma_{1}^{2}}\right)}}{{\left({\frac{{12\sigma_{w}^{2}}}{{r(r+1)(r+2)s^{2}}}+\sigma_{1}^{2}}\right)\left({\sigma_{0}^{2}+\frac{{2\sigma_{w}^{2}\left({r(1+2r)s^{2}+6rst_{0i}+6t_{0i}^{2}}\right)}}{{r(r+1)(r+2)s^{2}}}}\right)-\left({\sigma_{01}-\frac{{6\sigma_{w}^{2}(rs+2t_{0i})}}{{r(r+1)(r+2)s^{2}}}}\right)^{2}}},

c(t_{0i})=\frac{{\left({-\sigma_{01}+\frac{{6\sigma_{w}^{2}(rs+2t_{0i})}}{{r(r+1)(r+2)s^{2}}}}\right)}}{{\left({\frac{{12\sigma_{w}^{2}}}{{r(r+1)(r+2)s^{2}}}+\sigma_{1}^{2}}\right)\left({\sigma_{0}^{2}+\frac{{2\sigma_{w}^{2}\left({r(1+2r)s^{2}+6rst_{0i}+6t_{0i}^{2}}\right)}}{{r(r+1)(r+2)s^{2}}}}\right)-\left({\sigma_{01}-\frac{{6\sigma_{w}^{2}(rs+2t_{0i}}}{{r(r+1)(r+2)s^{2}}}}\right)^{2}}},

d(t_{0i})=\frac{{\left({\sigma_{0}^{2}+\frac{{2\sigma_{w}^{2}\left({r(1+2r)s^{2}+6rst_{0i}+6t_{0i}^{2}}\right)}}{{r(r+1)(r+2)s^{2}}}}\right)}}{{\left({\frac{{12\sigma_{w}^{2}}}{{r(r+1)(r+2)s^{2}}}+\sigma_{1}^{2}}\right)\left({\sigma_{0}^{2}+\frac{{2\sigma_{w}^{2}\left({r(1+2r)s^{2}+6rst_{0i}+6t_{0i}^{2}}\right)}}{{r(r+1)(r+2)s^{2}}}}\right)-\left({\sigma_{01}-\frac{{6\sigma_{w}^{2}(rs+2t_{0i}}}{{r(r+1)(r+2)s^{2}}}}\right)^{2}}}.

Pre- and post-multiplying by
{\mathbf{W}}_{i}
we get

{\mathbf{X}}_{i}^{\prime}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}{\mathbf{X}}_{i}={\mathbf{W}}_{i}^{\prime}\left({\left({{\mathbf{Z^{\prime}Z}}}\right)^{-1}\sigma_{within}^{2}+{\mathbf{D}}}\right)^{-1}{\mathbf{W}}_{i}=\\
\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{a(t_{0i})}&{c(t_{0i})}&{k_{i}a(t_{0i})}&{k_{i}c(t_{0i})}\\
{c(t_{0i})}&{d(t_{0i})}&{k_{i}c(t_{0i})}&{k_{i}d(t_{0i})}\\
{k_{i}a(t_{0i})}&{k_{i}c(t_{0i})}&{k_{i}a(t_{0i})}&{k_{i}c(t_{0i})}\\
{k_{i}c(t_{0i})}&{k_{i}d(t_{0i})}&{k_{i}c(t_{0i})}&{k_{i}d(t_{0i})}\\
\end{array}}\right).

Now,

\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbf{X}}_{i}^{\prime}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}{\mathbf{X}}_{i}}\right]=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}{\mathbb{E}\left[{a(t_{0i})}\right]}&{\mathbb{E}\left[{c(t_{0i})}\right]}&{\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}a(t_{0i})}\right]}&{\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}c(t_{0i})}\right]}\\
{\mathbb{E}\left[{c(t_{0i})}\right]}&{\mathbb{E}\left[{d(t_{0i})}\right]}&{\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}c(t_{0i})}\right]}&{\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}d(t_{0i})}\right]}\\
{\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}a(t_{0i})}\right]}&{\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}c(t_{0i})}\right]}&{\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}a(t_{0i})}\right]}&{\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}c(t_{0i})}\right]}\\
{\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}c(t_{0i})}\right]}&{\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}d(t_{0i})}\right]}&{\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}c(t_{0i})}\right]}&{\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}d(t_{0i})}\right]}\\
\end{array}}\right).

To compute the expected values in the matrix, we need to know the distribution of
t_{0i}
and the joint distribution of
\left({t_{0i},k_{i}}\right)
. We assume that the distribution of
t_{0i}
has mean zero and variance
V(t_{0})
and that
k_{i}
follows a Bernoulli distribution with probability of success
p_{e}
. Additionally, we assume that within each exposure group,
t_{0i}
is normally distributed with the same variance. In Appendix
A.1.2
we deduced the means of
t_{0i}
in each of the exposure groups as

\mathbb{E}\left({t_{0}|k=1}\right)=\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{{(1-p_{e})}}{{p_{e}}}}\sqrt{V\left({t_{0}}\right)}

and

\mathbb{E}\left[{t_{0}|k=1}\right]=-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{{p_{e}}}{{(1-p_{e})}}}\sqrt{V\left({t_{0}}\right)}.

Also, using the results from Appendix
A.1.2
, we can deduce that the common variance in the two groups is

V\left({t_{0}|k=1}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left({t_{0}^{2}|k=1}\right)-\left[{\mathbb{E}\left({t_{0}|k=1}\right)}\right]^{2}\\
=\frac{{V\left({t_{0}}\right)\left[{p_{e}+\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}(1-2p_{e})}\right]}}{{p_{e}}}-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}\frac{{(1-p_{e})}}{{p_{e}}}V\left({t_{0}}\right)=V\left({t_{0}}\right)\left({1-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}}\right).

Therefore, we can write

f(t_{0i}|k_{i}=1)=\\
\frac{1}{{\sqrt{2\pi V\left({t_{0}}\right)\left({1-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}}\right)}}}\exp\left[{\frac{{-1}}{{2V\left({t_{0}}\right)\left({1-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}}\right)}}\left({t_{0i}-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{{(1-p_{e})}}{{p_{e}}}}\sqrt{V\left({t_{0}}\right)}}\right)^{2}}\right]

and

f(t_{0i}|k_{i}=0)=\\
\frac{1}{{\sqrt{2\pi V\left({t_{0}}\right)\left({1-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}}\right)}}}\exp\left[{\frac{{-1}}{{2V\left({t_{0}}\right)\left({1-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}}\right)}}\left({t_{0i}+\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{{p_{e}}}{{(1-p_{e})}}}\sqrt{V\left({t_{0}}\right)}}\right)^{2}}\right].

We can write this conditional distribution as

f(t_{0i}|k_{i})=\frac{1}{{\sqrt{2\pi V\left({t_{0}}\right)\left({1-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}}\right)}}}\\
\exp\left[{\frac{{-1}}{{2V\left({t_{0}}\right)\left({1-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}}\right)}}\left({t_{0i}-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}\sqrt{V\left({t_{0}}\right)}(-1)^{1-k_{i}}(1-p_{e})^{k_{i}-\frac{1}{2}}p_{e}^{\frac{1}{2}-k_{i}}}\right)^{2}}\right].

Then, the joint distribution is

f(t_{0i},k_{i})$ $=\frac{{p_{e}^{k_{i}}(1-p_{e})^{1-k_{i}}}}{{\sqrt{2\pi V\left({t_{0}}\right)\left({1-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}}\right)}}}\\
\exp\left[{\frac{{-1}}{{2V\left({t_{0}}\right)\left({1-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}}\right)}}\left({t_{0i}-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}\sqrt{V\left({t_{0}}\right)}(-1)^{1-k_{i}}(1-p_{e})^{k_{i}-\frac{1}{2}}p_{e}^{\frac{1}{2}-k_{i}}}\right)^{2}}\right].

The marginal distribution of
t_{0i}
is a mixture of two normals,

f(t_{0i})=(1-p_{e})f(t_{0i}|k_{i}=0)+p_{e}f(t_{0i}|k_{i}=1).

Then, we can derive

\mathbb{E}\left[{a(t_{0i})}\right]=\int{a(t_{0i})f(t_{0i})}=(1-p_{e})\int{a(t_{0i})f(t_{0i}|k_{i}=0)}+p_{e}\int{a(t_{0i})f(t_{0i}|k_{i}=1)},

and equivalently for
\mathbb{E}\left[{c(t_{0i})}\right]
and
\mathbb{E}\left[{d(t_{0i})}\right]
. We can also derive

\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}a(t_{0i})}\right]=\int{\int{k_{i}a(t_{0i})f(t_{0i},k_{i})dk_{i}dt_{0i}}}=\sum\limits_{k_{i}=0,1}{\int{k_{i}a(t_{0i})f(t_{0i},k_{i})dt_{0i}}}=\\
\int{a(t_{0i})f(t_{0i},1)dt_{0i}}=\int{a(t_{0i})f(t_{0i},1)dt_{0i}}=\\
\int a(t_{0i})\frac{{p_{e}}}{{\sqrt{2\pi V\left({t_{0}}\right)\left({1-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}}\right)}}}\\
\exp\left[{\frac{{-1}}{{2V\left({t_{0}}\right)\left({1-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}^{2}}\right)}}\left({t_{0i}-\rho_{\operatorname{e},t_{0}}\sqrt{V\left({t_{0}}\right)}(1-p_{e})^{\frac{1}{2}}p_{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\right)^{2}}\right]dt_{0i}\\
=p_{e}\int{a(t_{0i})f(t_{0i}|k=1)dt_{0i}},

and equivalently for
\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}c(t_{0i})}\right]
and
\mathbb{E}\left[{k_{i}d(t_{0i})}\right]
.
Using the expressions derived here, our program computes the expectations numerically to obtain
\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbf{X}}_{i}^{\prime}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}{\mathbf{X}}_{i}}\right]
, then it inverts this matrix and extracts the [4,4] component, which will be
Var\left({\hat{\gamma}_{3}}\right)
. For CMD, the procedure is exactly the same but using the matrix

{\mathbf{W}}_{i}=\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}c}1&0&{k_{i}}\\
0&1&0\\
\end{array}}\right).

A.9
Proof that
r_{opt}
is the same for both the cost constraint and the power constraint, and reduces to the solution to the unconstrained problem (
4.2
), but
N_{opt}
depends upon the constraint

The power optimization problem is

\mathop{Max}\limits_{r}\;\Phi\left[{\frac{{\sqrt{N}\left|{\left({{\mathbf{c^{\prime}B}}}\right)_{H_{A}}}\right|}}{{\sqrt{{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}(r){\mathbf{c}}}}}-z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right]{\text{ subject to }}COST=Nc_{1}+\frac{{Nrc_{1}}}{\kappa}.

The cost constraint,

N=\frac{{\kappa COST}}{{c_{1}(\kappa+r)}},

can be plugged in the optimization function to obtain the unconstrained problem

\mathop{Max}\limits_{r}\;\Phi\left[{\frac{{\sqrt{\frac{{\kappa COST}}{{c_{1}(\kappa+r)}}}\left|{\left({{\mathbf{c^{\prime}B}}}\right)_{H_{A}}}\right|}}{{\sqrt{{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}(r){\mathbf{c}}}}}-z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right].

Since
\Phi
is a monotone function, this is equivalent to

\mathop{Max}\limits_{r}\;\frac{{\sqrt{\frac{{\kappa COST}}{{c_{1}(\kappa+r)}}}\left|{\left({{\mathbf{c^{\prime}B}}}\right)_{H_{A}}}\right|}}{{\sqrt{{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}(r){\mathbf{c}}}}}-z_{1-\alpha/2}.

Removing positive constant terms with respect to
r
, it is equivalent to

\mathop{Max}\limits_{r}\;\frac{1}{{(\kappa+r){\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}(r){\mathbf{c}}}},

which is in turn equivalent to
\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\;(\kappa+r){\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}(r){\mathbf{c}}
. Once
r_{opt}
is found solving this minimization problem,
N_{opt}
would be

N_{opt}=\frac{{\kappa\,COST}}{{c_{1}\left({\kappa+r_{opt}}\right)}}.

The cost optimization problem is

\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\;Nc_{1}+\frac{{Nrc_{1}}}{\kappa}{\text{ subject to }}\Phi\left[{\frac{{\sqrt{N}\left|{\left({{\mathbf{c^{\prime}B}}}\right)_{H_{A}}}\right|}}{{\sqrt{{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}(r){\mathbf{c}}}}}-z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right]=\pi.

Noting that

Nc_{1}+\frac{{Nrc_{1}}}{\kappa}=Nc_{1}\left({\frac{{\kappa+r}}{\kappa}}\right)

and that from the power constraint

N=\frac{{\left({{\mathbf{c^{\prime}}}\,{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{\rm B}(r)\,{\mathbf{c}}}\right)\left({z_{1-\alpha/2}+z_{\pi}}\right)^{2}}}{{\left({\left({{\mathbf{c^{\prime}{\rm B}}}}\right)_{H_{A}}}\right)^{2}}},

this is equivalent to the unconstrained problem

\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\;\frac{{({\mathbf{c^{\prime}}}\,{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{\rm B}(r)\,{\mathbf{c}})\left({z_{1-\alpha/2}+z_{\pi}}\right)^{2}}}{{\left({\left({{\mathbf{c^{\prime}{\rm B}}}}\right)_{H_{A}}}\right)^{2}}}c_{1}\left({\frac{{\kappa+r}}{\kappa}}\right).

Removing positive constant terms with respect to
r
, the problem becomes
\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\;\left({\kappa+r}\right)({\mathbf{c^{\prime}}}\,{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{\rm B}(r)\,{\mathbf{c}})
, which is equivalent to the minimization problem obtained before. Thus, given
\kappa
,
{\mathbf{c}}
and
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{\rm B}(r)
, the same
r_{opt}
maximizes power and minimizes cost. For the cost problem, once
r_{opt}
is found solving the minimization problem,

N_{opt}=\frac{{\left({{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}(r_{opt}){\mathbf{c}}}\right)\,\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}}}{{({\mathbf{c^{\prime}{\rm B}}}_{H_{A}})^{2}}}.

A.10
Optimal
r
under LDD and fixed
s
, for CS

The optimal
r
solves
\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\;(\kappa+r){\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
(Appendix
A.9
). Plugging in the appropriate value for
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
from Table
1
, the problem under LDD, CS and fixed
s
is

\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\;(\kappa+r)\frac{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho)}}{{p_{e}(1-p_{e})s^{2}r(r+1)(r+2)}}.

Removing positive constant terms with respect to
r
, this problem becomes

\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\hskip 10.00002ptF(r)=\frac{{(\kappa+r)}}{{r\;(r+1)(r+2)}}.

Since

\frac{{\partial F}}{{\partial r}}=\frac{{-2\kappa-6\kappa r-3r^{2}-3\kappa r^{2}-2r^{3}}}{{r^{2}(r+1)^{2}(r+2)^{2}}}<0\;\forall\kappa,

F(r)
decreases as
r
increases, and
r_{opt}\to\infty
subject to the cost constraint.

A.11
r_{opt}
under LDD, RS and fixed
s

The optimal
r
solves
\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\;(\kappa+r){\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
(Appendix
A.9
). Plugging in the appropriate value for
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
from Table
1
, the problem under LDD, RS and fixed
s
is

\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\;(\kappa+r)\left({\frac{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho_{t_{0}})}}{{s^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})}}}\right)\left({\frac{1}{{r(r+1)(r+2)}}+\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{1}{{\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}}\right).

Removing positive constant terms with respect to
r
, this problem becomes

\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\hskip 10.00002ptG(r)=(\kappa+r)\left({\frac{1}{{\,r(r+1)(r+2)}}+\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{1}{{\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}}\right).

The solution,
r_{opt}
, solves

\frac{{\partial G}}{{\partial r}}=\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{1}{{\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}+\frac{{-2\kappa-6\kappa r-3r^{2}-3\kappa r^{2}-2r^{3}}}{{r^{2}(r+1)^{2}(r+2)^{2}}}\\
=\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{1}{{\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}+\frac{{\partial F}}{{\partial r}}=0,

where
\frac{{\partial F}}{{\partial r}}
is the derivative of the objective function
F(r)
for the analogous problem under compound symmetry (Appendix
A.10
). We showed in Appendix
A.10
that
\frac{{\partial F}}{{\partial r}}
is always negative, and since

\frac{{\partial^{2}F}}{{\partial r^{2}}}=\frac{{2\left({4\kappa+18\kappa r+33\kappa r^{2}+7r^{3}+24\kappa r^{3}+9r^{4}+6\kappa r^{4}+3r^{5}}\right)}}{{r^{3}\left({r+1}\right)^{3}(r+2)^{3}}}\geqslant 0,

\frac{{\partial F}}{{\partial r}}
is also an increasing function of
r
. In addition,
\mathop{\lim}\limits_{r\to\infty}\frac{{\partial F}}{{\partial r}}=0^{-}
. Since
\frac{{\partial G}}{{\partial r}}
is
\frac{{\partial F}}{{\partial r}}
plus a constant,
\frac{{\partial G}}{{\partial r}}
will equal 0 at some interior point of
r
between 1 and
\infty
. Since

\frac{{\partial^{2}G}}{{\partial r^{2}}}=\frac{{2\left({4\kappa+18\kappa r+33\kappa r^{2}+7r^{3}+24\kappa r^{3}+9r^{4}+6\kappa r^{4}+3r^{5}}\right)}}{{r^{3}\left({r+1}\right)^{3}(r+2)^{3}}}\geqslant 0

for all
r>0
,
G(r)
is convex and the point that solves
\frac{{\partial G}}{{\partial r}}=0
is a global minimum and therefore it is
r_{opt}
.
Now,

\frac{{\partial G}}{{\partial r}}=0\Leftrightarrow\kappa=\frac{{r_{opt}^{2}\left({-(3+2r_{opt})\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)+(r_{opt}+1)^{2}(r_{opt}+2)^{2}\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)}}{{\,\left({2+6r_{opt}+3r_{opt}^{2}}\right)\tilde{r}(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}.

Figure
8
of the paper shows
r_{opt}
for several values of
\kappa
and
\rho_{b_{1},s,\tilde{r}}
.

A.12
\left({N_{opt},r_{opt}}\right)
under LDD, CS and fixed
\tau

As shown in Appendix
A.9
, the optimal
r
solves
\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\;(\kappa+r){\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
Plugging in the appropriate value for
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
from Table
1
, the problem under LDD, CS and fixed
\tau
is

\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\;(\kappa+r)\frac{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho)r}}{{p_{e}(1-p_{e})\tau^{2}(r+1)(r+2)}}.

Removing positive constant terms with respect to
r
, this problem becomes

\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\hskip 10.00002ptH(r)=\frac{{(\kappa+r)\,r}}{{(r+1)(r+2)}}.

Taking derivatives with respect to
r
,
r_{opt}
solves

\frac{{\partial H}}{{\partial r}}=\frac{{(3-\kappa)r^{2}+4r+2\kappa}}{{\left({r+1}\right)^{2}(r+2)^{2}}}=0.

For
\kappa<3
the derivative is positive. Therefore, when
\kappa<3

H(r)
increases with r and, consequently, the minimum is at
r=1
. If
\kappa>3
, the derivative equals 0 at

r=\frac{{2\pm\sqrt{2}\sqrt{2-3\kappa+\kappa^{2}}}}{{\kappa-3}},

which gives a positive solution only at

r=\frac{{2+\sqrt{2}\sqrt{2-3\kappa+\kappa^{2}}}}{{\kappa-3}}.

Now, we need to check whether at this point there is a maximum or a minimum of
H(r)
. The second derivative of
H(r)
is

\frac{{\partial^{2}H}}{{\partial r^{2}}}=\frac{{2\left({4-6\kappa-6\kappa r-6r^{2}-3r^{3}+\kappa r^{3}}\right)}}{{(r+1)^{3}(r+2)^{3}}}.

We evaluated the second derivative at the point

r=\frac{{2+\sqrt{2}\sqrt{2-3\kappa+\kappa^{2}}}}{{\kappa-3}}

with Mathematica
[
Wolfram:2005
]
and obtained

\left({24+\frac{{3\sqrt{2}}}{{\sqrt{(\kappa-2)(\kappa-1)}}}}\right)\kappa-17\sqrt{2}\sqrt{(\kappa-2)(\kappa-1)}-\frac{{7\sqrt{2}}}{{\sqrt{(\kappa-2)(\kappa-1)}}}-40.

This expression can be proven to be negative for all
\kappa>3
. Therefore,
H(r)
has a maximum at

r=\frac{{2+\sqrt{2}\sqrt{2-3\kappa+\kappa^{2}}}}{{\kappa-3}},

while we were looking for a minimum. Since this is the only local maximum or minimum of
H(r)
, the global minimum of
H(r)
will be at
r=1
or at
r=\infty
. The global minimum will be at
r=\infty
if we can find a value of r such that

H(r)<H(1)=\frac{{(1+\kappa)}}{6}.

With a little bit of algebra , we get

H(r)=\frac{{r(r+\kappa)}}{{(r+1)(r+2)}}<\frac{{(1+\kappa)}}{6}\Leftrightarrow r^{2}(\kappa-5)+r(-3\kappa+3)+2(\kappa+1)>0,

which has roots at
r=1
and
{\text{ }}r=\frac{{2(\kappa+1)}}{{\kappa-5}}
. If
\kappa<5
, then
{\text{ }}r=\frac{{2(\kappa+1)}}{{\kappa-5}}<0
, outside of its valid range. The global minimum is then
r_{opt}=1
,

N_{opt}=\frac{{\kappa COST}}{{c_{1}(\kappa+1)}}

or

N_{opt}=\frac{{2\sigma^{2}(1-\rho)\,\left({z_{\pi}+z_{1-\alpha/2}}\right)^{2}}}{{\tau^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})\gamma_{3}^{2}}}

for the power maximization or cost minimization problems, respectively. If
\kappa>5
, then
r=\frac{{2(\kappa+1)}}{{\kappa-5}}>1
within the range, so taking
r
as large as possible subject to the cost constraint,

r_{opt}=\kappa\left({\frac{{COST}}{{c_{1}}}-1}\right)

and
N_{opt}=1
. Under a power constraint, one would find the smallest
r
that satisfies the power constraint and set
N_{opt}=1
. In reality, the investigator will set
r
as large as is feasible, and then find
N_{opt}
to satisfy the cost or power constraint.

A.13
\left({N_{opt},r_{opt}}\right)
under LDD, RS and fixed
\tau

The optimal
r
solves
\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\;(\kappa+r){\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
(Appendix
A.9
). Plugging in the appropriate value for
{\mathbf{c^{\prime}\Sigma}}_{\rm B}{\mathbf{c}}
from Table
1
, the problem under LDD, RS and fixed
\tau
is

\mathop{Min}\limits_{r}\;(\kappa+r)\left({\frac{{12\sigma^{2}(1-\rho_{t_{0}})}}{{s^{2}p_{e}(1-p_{e})}}}\right)\left({\frac{r}{{(r+1)(r+2)}}+\left({\frac{{\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}}{{1-\rho_{b_{1},\tau,\tilde{r}}}}}\right)\frac{{\tilde{r}}}{{(\tilde{r}+1)(\tilde{r}+2)}}}\right).

Removing positive constant terms with respect to
r
, this problem becomes
